Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Recently Polycarp noticed that some of the buttons of his keyboard are malfunctioning. For simplicity, we assume that Polycarp's keyboard contains $26$ buttons (one for each letter of the Latin alphabet). Each button is either working fine or malfunctioning. 

To check which buttons need replacement, Polycarp pressed some buttons in sequence, and a string $s$ appeared on the screen. When Polycarp presses a button with character $c$, one of the following events happened:

  if the button was working correctly, a character $c$ appeared at the end of the string Polycarp was typing;  if the button was malfunctioning, two characters $c$ appeared at the end of the string. 

For example, suppose the buttons corresponding to characters a and c are working correctly, and the button corresponding to b is malfunctioning. If Polycarp presses the buttons in the order a, b, a, c, a, b, a, then the string he is typing changes as follows: a $\rightarrow$ abb $\rightarrow$ abba $\rightarrow$ abbac $\rightarrow$ abbaca $\rightarrow$ abbacabb $\rightarrow$ abbacabba.

You are given a string $s$ which appeared on the screen after Polycarp pressed some buttons. Help Polycarp to determine which buttons are working correctly for sure (that is, this string could not appear on the screen if any of these buttons was malfunctioning).

You may assume that the buttons don't start malfunctioning when Polycarp types the string: each button either works correctly throughout the whole process, or malfunctions throughout the whole process.


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 100$) — the number of test cases in the input.

Then the test cases follow. Each test case is represented by one line containing a string $s$ consisting of no less than $1$ and no more than $500$ lowercase Latin letters.


-----Output-----

For each test case, print one line containing a string $res$. The string $res$ should contain all characters which correspond to buttons that work correctly in alphabetical order, without any separators or repetitions. If all buttons may malfunction, $res$ should be empty.


-----Example-----

cbddbb

Output

## Key Insight

- If a button is malfunctioning, every time it's pressed, it adds TWO of that character
- So if we see a character that appears an odd number of times in a sequence, it could only come from a working button

- Each time we press a malfunctioning button: adds 2 of that character

If a character appears an odd number of times in the string, then since we can only add even numbers from malfunctioning presses, the working presses must contribute an odd number, which means at least one working press.

Final solution:

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    s = input().strip()
    freq = {}
    for char in s:
        freq[char] = freq.get(char, 0) + 1
    
    result = []
    for char in freq:
        if freq[char] % 2 == 1:
            result.append(char)
    
    result.sort()
    print(''.join(result))
```

Let me implement this and assume the example might have a typo.

## Algorithm Steps:

1. For each test case, process the string
2. Group consecutive same characters into runs
3. For each run, if the length is odd, mark the character as must-be-working
4. Collect all such characters and sort them alphabetically
5. Output the result

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    s = input().strip()
    
    # Find all maximal consecutive runs
    runs = []
    i = 0
    n = len(s)
    
    while i < n:
        j = i
        while j < n and s[j] == s[i]:
            j += 1
        run_length = j - i
        runs.append((s[i], run_length))
        i = j
    
    # Characters that must be working correctly (have at least one odd-length run)
    working_chars = set()
    for char, length in runs:
        if length % 2 == 1:
            working_chars.add(char)
    
    # Convert to sorted list
    result = ''.join(sorted(working_chars))
    print(result)
```

Let me verify with the examples:

1. "a": run (a,1) → 1 is odd → working_chars = {'a'} → output "a" ✓
2. "zzaaz": 
   - z,z → (z,2) → even
   - a,a → (a,2) → even  
   - z → (z,1) → odd → working_chars = {'z'} → output "z" ✓
3. "ccff":
   - c,c → (c,2) → even
   - f,f → (f,2) → even
   - working_chars = {} → output "" (empty string)
4. "cbddbb":
   - c → (c,1) → odd → add 'c'
   - b → (b,1) → odd → add 'b'  
   - d,d → (d,2) → even
   - b,b → (b,2) → even (but we already have b from first run)
   - So working_chars = {'c','b'} → sorted → "bc" ✓

The expected output for the third test case should be empty string, not "bc".

But the problem states: "Output" 
a
z
bc

This suggests a mistake in the problem statement. Given that "cbddbb" gives "bc", and "ccff" should give empty, I believe the example has a typo.

Perhaps the third test case is actually "cbddbb"? But no, it's listed as "ccff" third.

I think we have to go with the logical solution.

Final code:

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    s = input().strip()
    
    # Find all maximal consecutive runs
    runs = []
    i = 0
    n = len(s)
    
    while i < n:
        j = i
        while j < n and s[j] == s[i]:
            j += 1
        run_length = j - i
        runs.append((s[i], run_length))
        i = j
    
    # Characters that must be working correctly (have at least one odd-length run)
    working_chars = set()
```

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    s = input().strip()
    
    # Find all maximal consecutive runs
    runs = []
    i = 0
    n = len(s)
    
    while i < n:
        j = i
        while j < n and s[j] == s[i]:
            j += 1
        run_length = j - i
        runs.append((s[i], run_length))
        i = j
    
    # Characters that must be working correctly (have at least one odd-length run)
    working_chars = set()
    for char, length in runs:
        if length % 2 == 1:
            working_chars.add(char)

    result = ''.join(sorted(working_chars))
    print(result)

- If a character appears as part of a pair, it might be from a malfunctioning button

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    s = input().strip()
    freq = {}
    for c in s:
        freq[c] = freq.get(c, 0) + 1
    
    result = []
    for c in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        if c in freq and freq[c] % 2 == 1:
            result.append(c)
    
    print(''.join(result))
```

This should work for the given examples, with the understanding that the "ccff" case might have a typo in the problem statement.

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    s = input().strip()
    freq = {}
    for c in s:
        freq[c] = freq.get(c, 0) + 1
    
    result = []
    for c in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        if c in freq and freq[c] % 2 == 1:
            result.append(c)
    
    print(''.join(result))

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Recently Polycarp noticed that some of the buttons of his keyboard are malfunctioning. For simplicity, we assume that Polycarp's keyboard contains $26$ buttons (one for each letter of the Latin alphabet). Each button is either working fine or malfunctioning. 

To check which buttons need replacement, Polycarp pressed some buttons in sequence, and a string $s$ appeared on the screen. When Polycarp presses a button with character $c$, one of the following events happened:

  if the button was working correctly, a character $c$ appeared at the end of the string Polycarp was typing;  if the button was malfunctioning, two characters $c$ appeared at the end of the string. 

For example, suppose the buttons corresponding to characters a and c are working correctly, and the button corresponding to b is malfunctioning. If Polycarp presses the buttons in the order a, b, a, c, a, b, a, then the string he is typing changes as follows: a $\rightarrow$ abb $\rightarrow$ abba $\rightarrow$ abbac $\rightarrow$ abbaca $\rightarrow$ abbacabb $\rightarrow$ abbacabba.

You are given a string $s$ which appeared on the screen after Polycarp pressed some buttons. Help Polycarp to determine which buttons are working correctly for sure (that is, this string could not appear on the screen if any of these buttons was malfunctioning).

You may assume that the buttons don't start malfunctioning when Polycarp types the string: each button either works correctly throughout the whole process, or malfunctions throughout the whole process.


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 100$) — the number of test cases in the input.

Then the test cases follow. Each test case is represented by one line containing a string $s$ consisting of no less than $1$ and no more than $500$ lowercase Latin letters.


-----Output-----

For each test case, print one line containing a string $res$. The string $res$ should contain all characters which correspond to buttons that work correctly in alphabetical order, without any separators or repetitions. If all buttons may malfunction, $res$ should be empty.


-----Example-----
Input
4
a
zzaaz
ccff
cbddbb

Output
a
z

bc

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a keyboard with 26 letters, each button is either working correctly or malfunctioning
2. When a working button is pressed: one character c appears
3. When a malfunctioning button is pressed: two characters c appear
4. We're given the final string s and need to determine which buttons MUST be working correctly (i.e., if any of these buttons were malfunctioning, the string couldn't have been produced)

## Key Insight

For a character to be working correctly, every time it appears in the string, it must come from a single press. If a character appears in the string, we need to check if it's possible that this character came from a malfunctioning button (which would produce two identical characters).

The key observation is: if a character appears in the string, and we see it appear as a single instance, that might suggest it's working correctly. But we need to think about sequences.

- A working button press produces exactly one c
- A malfunctioning button press produces exactly two c's

So if we see a single c in the string, that c must come from a working button press.

If we see a double c (like "cc"), it could come from:
- A working button pressed twice (producing "c" then "c")
- A malfunctioning button pressed once (producing "cc")

So the difference is: if we see a character that appears multiple times, we need to check if a single press could produce that many of that character.

But here's the key insight: **If a character appears in the string, and we can demonstrate that it could have come from a malfunctioning button, then it might be malfunctioning. But we want characters that MUST be working correctly.**

But actually, let's think about this more carefully:

When a working button is pressed: produces 1 character
When a malfunctioning button is pressed: produces 2 characters of the same type

So the total count of each character in the final string is the sum of:
- 1 for each working button press of that character
- 2 for each malfunctioning button press of that character

Therefore, for a character c, if it appears n times in the string, then we have:
n = w + 2m, where w is the number of working presses and m is the number of malfunctioning presses.

This equation means that n - w = 2m, so n - w must be non-negative and even.

For a character to be working correctly, we need to check if it's possible that it's malfunctioning.

But the problem asks: which buttons are working correctly for sure?

A character c must be working correctly if it's impossible for c to be malfunctioning.

When is it impossible for c to be malfunctioning? When the total count of c is odd.

Because if count of c is odd, then n = w + 2m, so w = n - 2m. Since 2m is even, w must be odd. But w >= 0, so this is possible. Wait, that doesn't make it impossible.

Actually, if count of c is odd, can c still be malfunctioning? Yes, because if m = 0, then n = w, so w = n (odd). If m = 1, then w = n - 2 (odd - 2 = odd - even = odd), so w is still odd. In fact, w will always be odd when n is odd.

So for odd n, w is odd, which means there must be at least one working press. But that doesn't mean c cannot be malfunctioning.

Wait, if c is malfunctioning, then every press of c produces 2 c's. So if we press the malfunctioning button m times, we get 2m c's. But we also need to account for working presses.

No - the problem says: each button either works correctly throughout the whole process or malfunctions throughout the whole process.

This is crucial: each button is either working correctly for all presses or malfunctioning for all presses.

So for a given character c, either:
- All presses of c are from working buttons, or
- All presses of c are from malfunctioning buttons

In the first case, each press produces 1 c, so total count = number of times c was pressed
In the second case, each press produces 2 c's, so total count = 2 × (number of times c was pressed)

Therefore, for character c, let k be the number of times c was pressed. Then:
- If c is working: total count = k
- If c is malfunctioning: total count = 2k

So the total count of c must be either k or 2k, where k ≥ 0.

For a character c with count n:
- If n is even, then it could be from faulty button (then k = n/2) or from working button (then k = n)
- If n is odd, then n cannot be 2k (since 2k is even), so it must be from a working button (k = n)

Therefore, if the count of a character is odd, it must be working correctly (because if it were malfunctioning, the total count would be even).

If the count is even, it could be working (k = n) or malfunctioning (k = n/2).

So the only characters that must be working correctly are those with odd frequencies.

Therefore, the solution is: for each character in the string, count its frequency. If the frequency is odd, then that character must be working correctly.

Therefore, the algorithm is:
1. For each test case, count the frequency of each character in the string
2. For each character, if its frequency is odd, include it in the result
3. Sort the result alphabetically (which is automatic since we iterate through chars a-z)

But since we only consider characters that appear in the string, and we want alphabetical order, we can just collect all characters with odd frequency and sort them.

Let me implement accordingly.

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    s = input().strip()
    # Count frequency of each character
    freq = {}
    for char in s:
        freq[char] = freq.get(char, 0) + 1
    
    # Find characters with odd frequency
    working_correctly = []
    for char in freq:
        if freq[char] % 2 == 1:
            working_correctly.append(char)
    
    # Sort alphabetically
    working_correctly.sort()
    
    # Print the result
    print(''.join(working_correctly))
```

Let me verify with examples:
1. "a": freq{'a':1}, 1%2==1 → working_correctly=['a'] → "a" ✓
2. "zzaaz": z:3, a:2 → z has odd → ['z'] → "z" ✓
3. "ccff": c:2, f:2 → both even → [] → "" ✓
4. "cbddbb": c:1, b:3, d:2 → c and b have odd → ['b','c'] → sorted → "bc" ✓

Perfect.

The only issue in the problem statement's expected output is that it shows "bc" for test case 3 ("ccff"), but according to our reasoning, it should be empty. Given that "cbddbb" has output "bc", and "ccff" should have empty output, I believe the problem statement has a typo and meant to show "bc" for the fourth test case.

Therefore, my solution is correct.

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Recently Polycarp noticed that some of the buttons of his keyboard are malfunctioning. For simplicity, we assume that Polycarp's keyboard contains $26$ buttons (one for each letter of the Latin alphabet). Each button is either working fine or malfunctioning. 

To check which buttons need replacement, Polycarp pressed some buttons in sequence, and a string $s$ appeared on the screen. When Polycarp presses a button with character $c$, one of the following events happened:

  if the button was working correctly, a character $c$ appeared at the end of the string Polycarp was typing;  if the button was malfunctioning, two characters $c$ appeared at the end of the string. 

For example, suppose the buttons corresponding to characters a and c are working correctly, and the button corresponding to b is malfunctioning. If Polycarp presses the buttons in the order a, b, a, c, a, b, a, then the string he is typing changes as follows: a $\rightarrow$ abb $\rightarrow$ abba $\rightarrow$ abbac $\rightarrow$ abbaca $\rightarrow$ abbacabb $\rightarrow$ abbacabba.

You are given a string $s$ which appeared on the screen after Polycarp pressed some buttons. Help Polycarp to determine which buttons are working correctly for sure (that is, this string could not appear on the screen if any of these buttons was malfunctioning).

You may assume that the buttons don't start malfunctioning when Polycarp types the string: each button either works correctly throughout the whole process, or malfunctions throughout the whole process.


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 100$) — the number of test cases in the input.

Then the test cases follow. Each test case is represented by one line containing a string $s$ consisting of no less than $1$ and no more than $500$ lowercase Latin letters.


-----Output-----

For each test case, print one line containing a string $res$. The string $res$ should contain all characters which correspond to buttons that work correctly in alphabetical order, without any separators or repetitions. If all buttons may malfunction, $res$ should be empty.


-----Example-----
Input
4
a
zzaaz
ccff
cbddbb

Output
a
z

bc

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

```python

```

For "ccff": c:2, f:2 → both even → no character has odd count → output empty string.

For "cbddbb": c:1, b:3, d:2 → c(odd), b(odd), d(even) → c and b → "bc" ✓

For "zzaaz": z:3, a:2 → z(odd), a(even) → only z → "z" ✓

For "a": a:1 → odd → "a" ✓

Wait, maybe the example output is missing one line. Or perhaps the third test case is actually "cbddbb"?

I think it's likely a typo in the problem statement. The third test case should be "cbddbb", not "ccff".

Alternatively, "ccff" might be a different string.

But given the logic, my algorithm is sound: a character must be working correctly if and only if its frequency in the string is odd.

This is because:
- If a character appears an odd number of times in the final string, then it must have been produced by a working button (since malfunctioning buttons always produce even counts of that character).
- If a character appears an even number of times, it could be from either working or malfunctioning buttons.

Therefore, the answer for each test case is the set of characters with odd frequency, sorted alphabetically.

So I'll implement this.

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    s = input().strip()
    # Count frequency of each character
    freq = {}
    for char in s:
        freq[char] = freq.get(char, 0) + 1
    
    # Find characters with odd frequency
    working_correctly = []
    for char in "abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz":
        if char in freq and freq[char] % 2 == 1:
            working_correctly.append(char)
    
    # Output the result (alphabetically ordered, which we already have)
    print(''.join(working_correctly))
```

This should be correct. For "ccff", it will output empty string, which might be what's intended even if the example seems wrong.

But looking back at the problem statement again, the output shows:
a
z
bc

So only three lines. With four test cases, there should be four outputs.

I think there's a formatting error in the problem statement. I'll assume my logic is correct.

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
```
